[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F60.0, F23, F20.0, F31.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it's good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because this has been going on for months, and I feel like nobody is really listening to me. It’s about my husband’s family. They’ve been quietly interfering with our money and with my reputation, our good name, you know? Not in some obvious way where you can point to one big thing, but in these little things that keep happening.\n\nLike at work, I’m a billing clerk, so I notice numbers. I notice when something is off. Little billing errors come up, things that make me look careless, and people act like it’s just a mistake or they pull up records and say, “See, this doesn’t prove anything,” but I know how this connects. And then at church someone will suddenly be strange with me, like they’ve heard something or been told something, and I can feel the shift. It’s not random.\n\nI’ve tried to explain it, but people keep focusing on whether I can show one document that says his family did it. That’s not how they work. It’s quiet. It’s spread out. But I know it ties back to them, and I need someone to understand that instead of just brushing me off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one else is there?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not voices like you’re describing, like someone talking when nobody’s there. I hear people talk at work or at church, of course, and sometimes I wonder what they’ve been told, because the way they lower their voices or stop when I come near, it feels like there’s something behind it. But I wouldn’t say I’m hearing voices out of nowhere.\n\nIt’s more… I notice how people act, what changes, the little comments, the timing of things. That’s different from hearing a voice in an empty room. I know the difference.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your husband’s family has any special way of controlling events around you that other people would say is impossible?\n\nPatient: I don’t mean like… magic or something like that, if that’s what you’re asking. I’m not saying they have some impossible power. It’s more that they know how to work quietly, through people, through little situations, so by the time something happens it looks like nothing.\n\nLike with the money and the billing things at work — I’m a billing clerk, I know how records look when they’re right and when something is off. And when these little errors pop up, not huge enough for someone to say, “Here, this proves it,” but just enough to make me look careless, it’s hard for me to believe that’s all accidental. Then the same kind of thing happens socially, like at church, where someone who was perfectly normal with me suddenly acts cold or careful around me. People say, “Well, maybe they were just having a bad day,” but when it keeps lining up, you start to see the pattern.\n\nSo no, not controlling events in an impossible way. But influencing things, yes. Quietly. In ways other people don’t want to take seriously because they can’t put one piece of paper in front of me and say, “This is the proof.” But I know it ties back to my husband’s family. I’ve been living with it for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period when you felt unusually high, energized, or irritable for days at a time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “high.” I mean, I get upset, yes, especially when I feel like people are acting as if I’m making things up or when something happens and everyone wants to pretend it’s just a coincidence. That can make me feel keyed up, and I’ll think about it a lot because I’m trying to sort out what happened and why.\n\nBut if you mean like being unusually energized for days, like not myself, or feeling on top of the world or something… I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed that in that way. Irritable, maybe, but I think anyone would be irritable if they were dealing with this and nobody was taking it seriously. So I don’t know if that counts as what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Because this started, has your ability to do your job gone downhill?\n\nPatient: Not really downhill in the sense of, like, I haven’t been fired or written up or anything like that. I’m still going in, doing the billing work, keeping up with my regular responsibilities. I mean, I may double-check things more than I used to, because I don’t want anyone saying I made a mistake when I didn’t, but I still get the work done.\n\nWhere it’s affected things more is outside of that, honestly. My husband and I have argued more because he thinks I should just let it go, or he acts like I’m making something out of nothing, and that’s frustrating. And at church there have been some uncomfortable moments, because people seem to want me to stop bringing it up or pretend everything is fine. So socially, yes, it’s made things awkward. At home, yes, more tension. But my job itself — I’m still functioning, I’m still taking care of what I’m supposed to take care of.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been dealing with this situation with your husband’s family?\n\nPatient: It’s been about nine months now, I’d say. It really started after that trouble with my husband’s family over money, and then the expectations around the holidays — who was supposed to do what, pay for what, show up where, all of that. At first I thought it was just family tension, you know, something that would blow over, but it didn’t.\n\nSo… yes, about nine months. Long enough that it’s not just one misunderstanding or one bad week. It’s been going on long enough that I can look back and see when things shifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you had the feeling that people can read your thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think they’re reading my thoughts exactly. It’s not like I believe someone can hear words in my head or anything like that.\n\nWhat I do think is that people are picking up things or being told things somehow, because there are these little things that keep happening. Like with my husband’s family — for months I’ve been trying to explain that they’re quietly interfering with our money and how people see us. Then a billing error shows up at work, or someone at church suddenly acts strange toward me, like they’ve heard something or they’re looking at me differently, and everyone wants to say it’s unrelated. But it doesn’t feel unrelated to me.\n\nSo, no, not reading my thoughts. It’s more like they know things or are spreading things, and no matter what records people pull up, I can see how it ties back to them.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that something outside you was putting thoughts into your mind?\n\nPatient: No, not like that. I don’t feel like someone is putting thoughts into my head or controlling what I think. My thoughts feel like my own.\n\nWhat I keep trying to explain is different. It’s that for months now, I’ve been seeing how my husband’s family is quietly messing with our money and our good name. It’s not some random idea that just appeared in my head from outside — it’s from things that keep happening. Like little billing errors showing up at work, or someone at church suddenly acting funny toward me, like they’ve heard something about me or they’re treating me differently. And then people want to pull up records or explain it away like it’s all separate, but I know it ties back to them.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say thoughts are being put into my mind. It’s more that I can see what’s going on, and it’s frustrating because other people keep acting like I’m the only one noticing it.\n\nDoctor: When you talk, do people ever tell you that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one idea to another in a way that doesn’t make sense to them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in the sense that people tell me I’m not making sense. I can be long-winded, I know that. My husband has said I explain things with too much detail, and sometimes people want me to get to the point faster, especially at work when everyone’s busy and they just want the short version.\n\nBut I don’t think people say I jump around in a way that doesn’t make sense. I may go back and fill in details because I feel like if I leave something out, then the whole thing gets misunderstood. So I’ll start with one part and then remember another part that matters, and I’ll circle back. To me it all connects, but I know it can probably sound like I’m giving more than they asked for.\n\nSo… hard to follow, maybe because I talk too much sometimes. But not because my words don’t make sense, at least not that anyone has really told me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that other people were controlling your actions?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anyone is controlling my actions. I mean, I make my own decisions, I go to work, I do what I need to do, and I don’t feel like someone is moving me around or making me say or do things.\n\nSometimes I might feel pressured by people, or like other people’s behavior affects what I have to do next — like if someone creates a problem, then I have to respond to it — but that’s not the same as them controlling my body or my actions. I know when I choose to do something. So no, I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nDoctor: If someone shows you evidence that your husband’s family was not involved in one of these incidents, are you able to accept that for that incident?\n\nPatient: Well… I can look at whatever someone wants to show me, and I’m not saying every single paper in front of me is fake or anything like that. If there’s a record that says one particular charge was entered by this person or that person, I can understand what the record says for that one little piece.\n\nBut what frustrates me is people act like showing me one record should make me ignore the whole pattern. These things have been happening for months — little billing errors showing up at work, people suddenly acting strange toward me at church, the way our money and our reputation seem to keep getting touched in these quiet ways. So if someone says, “Here, this proves they weren’t involved in this one incident,” I might say, fine, maybe not directly on that one paper or in that one step. But that doesn’t mean they weren’t behind it somehow, or that it doesn’t still tie back to them.\n\nThat’s the part people don’t seem to understand. They want to separate everything out like it’s all random, but when you’re the one living it, you see how it connects. So I can accept a detail, maybe, but I don’t just drop the whole thing because someone pulls up a record.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in being around other people or in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, not exactly. I mean, I still do things. I get up and go to work, and I handle my billing clerk duties. I may be tired of dealing with certain people, but I’m not just sitting at home doing nothing. I keep the house going, I take care of what needs taking care of.\n\nAnd I still like my own things, like gardening. That’s actually calming for me, because plants don’t twist things around, you know? And painting, too — when I have the time and the right frame of mind, I still enjoy that. So I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in everything or stopped wanting to do anything.\n\nWhere it gets difficult is more when I’m around people and I try to warn them about what my in-laws are doing. That’s when it changes. People get uncomfortable, or they act like I’m being dramatic, and then it makes me not want to keep explaining myself over and over. So it’s not that I don’t care about people or activities anymore. It’s more that some situations become hard because of how people react when I bring up what’s been going on.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, have you generally tended to mistrust other people and suspect that they might be trying to harm or deceive you?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think I was always like this. I mean, I’ve never been stupid about people — if someone seems dishonest, I notice it — but I wasn’t going around suspecting everybody or thinking people were out to get me.\n\nThis really started about nine months ago, after all that trouble with my husband’s family over money and the holiday expectations. That’s when things changed. Before that, I could get annoyed with people like anyone else, but I didn’t have this constant feeling that I had to watch what they were doing or figure out what was behind things. It’s been since that situation with them that I started seeing how these things were connecting.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Emotionally flat? I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I still feel things, definitely. I get upset, I get frustrated, I can laugh if something is actually funny, and I still care about what’s happening around me.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F20.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it's good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because this has been going on for months, and I feel like nobody is really listening to me. It’s about my husband’s family. They’ve been quietly interfering with our money and with my reputation, our good name, you know? Not in some obvious way where you can point to one big thing, but in these little things that keep happening.\n\nLike at work, I’m a billing clerk, so I notice numbers. I notice when something is off. Little billing errors come up, things that make me look careless, and people act like it’s just a mistake or they pull up records and say, “See, this doesn’t prove anything,” but I know how this connects. And then at church someone will suddenly be strange with me, like they’ve heard something or been told something, and I can feel the shift. It’s not random.\n\nI’ve tried to explain it, but people keep focusing on whether I can show one document that says his family did it. That’s not how they work. It’s quiet. It’s spread out. But I know it ties back to them, and I need someone to understand that instead of just brushing me off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one else is there?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not voices like you’re describing, like someone talking when nobody’s there. I hear people talk at work or at church, of course, and sometimes I wonder what they’ve been told, because the way they lower their voices or stop when I come near, it feels like there’s something behind it. But I wouldn’t say I’m hearing voices out of nowhere.\n\nIt’s more… I notice how people act, what changes, the little comments, the timing of things. That’s different from hearing a voice in an empty room. I know the difference.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your husband’s family has any special way of controlling events around you that other people would say is impossible?\n\nPatient: I don’t mean like… magic or something like that, if that’s what you’re asking. I’m not saying they have some impossible power. It’s more that they know how to work quietly, through people, through little situations, so by the time something happens it looks like nothing.\n\nLike with the money and the billing things at work — I’m a billing clerk, I know how records look when they’re right and when something is off. And when these little errors pop up, not huge enough for someone to say, “Here, this proves it,” but just enough to make me look careless, it’s hard for me to believe that’s all accidental. Then the same kind of thing happens socially, like at church, where someone who was perfectly normal with me suddenly acts cold or careful around me. People say, “Well, maybe they were just having a bad day,” but when it keeps lining up, you start to see the pattern.\n\nSo no, not controlling events in an impossible way. But influencing things, yes. Quietly. In ways other people don’t want to take seriously because they can’t put one piece of paper in front of me and say, “This is the proof.” But I know it ties back to my husband’s family. I’ve been living with it for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period when you felt unusually high, energized, or irritable for days at a time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “high.” I mean, I get upset, yes, especially when I feel like people are acting as if I’m making things up or when something happens and everyone wants to pretend it’s just a coincidence. That can make me feel keyed up, and I’ll think about it a lot because I’m trying to sort out what happened and why.\n\nBut if you mean like being unusually energized for days, like not myself, or feeling on top of the world or something… I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed that in that way. Irritable, maybe, but I think anyone would be irritable if they were dealing with this and nobody was taking it seriously. So I don’t know if that counts as what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Because this started, has your ability to do your job gone downhill?\n\nPatient: Not really downhill in the sense of, like, I haven’t been fired or written up or anything like that. I’m still going in, doing the billing work, keeping up with my regular responsibilities. I mean, I may double-check things more than I used to, because I don’t want anyone saying I made a mistake when I didn’t, but I still get the work done.\n\nWhere it’s affected things more is outside of that, honestly. My husband and I have argued more because he thinks I should just let it go, or he acts like I’m making something out of nothing, and that’s frustrating. And at church there have been some uncomfortable moments, because people seem to want me to stop bringing it up or pretend everything is fine. So socially, yes, it’s made things awkward. At home, yes, more tension. But my job itself — I’m still functioning, I’m still taking care of what I’m supposed to take care of.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been dealing with this situation with your husband’s family?\n\nPatient: It’s been about nine months now, I’d say. It really started after that trouble with my husband’s family over money, and then the expectations around the holidays — who was supposed to do what, pay for what, show up where, all of that. At first I thought it was just family tension, you know, something that would blow over, but it didn’t.\n\nSo… yes, about nine months. Long enough that it’s not just one misunderstanding or one bad week. It’s been going on long enough that I can look back and see when things shifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you had the feeling that people can read your thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think they’re reading my thoughts exactly. It’s not like I believe someone can hear words in my head or anything like that.\n\nWhat I do think is that people are picking up things or being told things somehow, because there are these little things that keep happening. Like with my husband’s family — for months I’ve been trying to explain that they’re quietly interfering with our money and how people see us. Then a billing error shows up at work, or someone at church suddenly acts strange toward me, like they’ve heard something or they’re looking at me differently, and everyone wants to say it’s unrelated. But it doesn’t feel unrelated to me.\n\nSo, no, not reading my thoughts. It’s more like they know things or are spreading things, and no matter what records people pull up, I can see how it ties back to them.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that something outside you was putting thoughts into your mind?\n\nPatient: No, not like that. I don’t feel like someone is putting thoughts into my head or controlling what I think. My thoughts feel like my own.\n\nWhat I keep trying to explain is different. It’s that for months now, I’ve been seeing how my husband’s family is quietly messing with our money and our good name. It’s not some random idea that just appeared in my head from outside — it’s from things that keep happening. Like little billing errors showing up at work, or someone at church suddenly acting funny toward me, like they’ve heard something about me or they’re treating me differently. And then people want to pull up records or explain it away like it’s all separate, but I know it ties back to them.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say thoughts are being put into my mind. It’s more that I can see what’s going on, and it’s frustrating because other people keep acting like I’m the only one noticing it.\n\nDoctor: When you talk, do people ever tell you that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one idea to another in a way that doesn’t make sense to them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in the sense that people tell me I’m not making sense. I can be long-winded, I know that. My husband has said I explain things with too much detail, and sometimes people want me to get to the point faster, especially at work when everyone’s busy and they just want the short version.\n\nBut I don’t think people say I jump around in a way that doesn’t make sense. I may go back and fill in details because I feel like if I leave something out, then the whole thing gets misunderstood. So I’ll start with one part and then remember another part that matters, and I’ll circle back. To me it all connects, but I know it can probably sound like I’m giving more than they asked for.\n\nSo… hard to follow, maybe because I talk too much sometimes. But not because my words don’t make sense, at least not that anyone has really told me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that other people were controlling your actions?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anyone is controlling my actions. I mean, I make my own decisions, I go to work, I do what I need to do, and I don’t feel like someone is moving me around or making me say or do things.\n\nSometimes I might feel pressured by people, or like other people’s behavior affects what I have to do next — like if someone creates a problem, then I have to respond to it — but that’s not the same as them controlling my body or my actions. I know when I choose to do something. So no, I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nDoctor: If someone shows you evidence that your husband’s family was not involved in one of these incidents, are you able to accept that for that incident?\n\nPatient: Well… I can look at whatever someone wants to show me, and I’m not saying every single paper in front of me is fake or anything like that. I’ve been living with it for months.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period when you felt unusually high, energized, or irritable for days at a time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “high.” I mean, I get upset, yes, especially when I feel like people are acting as if I’m making things up or when something happens and everyone wants to pretend it’s just a coincidence. That can make me feel keyed up, and I’ll think about it a lot because I’m trying to sort out what happened and why.\n\nBut if you mean like being unusually energized for days, like not myself, or feeling on top of the world or something… I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed that in that way. Irritable, maybe, but I think anyone would be irritable if they were dealing with this and nobody was taking it seriously. So I don’t know if that counts as what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Because this started, has your ability to do your job gone downhill?\n\nPatient: Not really downhill in the sense of, like, I haven’t been fired or written up or anything like that. I’m still going in, doing the billing work, keeping up with my regular responsibilities. I mean, I may double-check things more than I used to, because I don’t want anyone saying I made a mistake when I didn’t, but I still get the work done.\n\nWhere it’s affected things more is outside of that, honestly. My husband and I have argued more because he thinks I should just let it go, or he acts like I’m making something out of nothing, and that’s frustrating. And at church there have been some uncomfortable moments, because people seem to want me to stop bringing it up or pretend everything is fine. So socially, yes, it’s made things awkward. At home, yes, more tension. But my job itself — I’m still functioning, I’m still taking care of what I’m supposed to take care of.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been dealing with this situation with your husband’s family?\n\nPatient: It’s been about nine months now, I’d say. It really started after that trouble with my husband’s family over money, and then the expectations around the holidays — who was supposed to do what, pay for what, show up where, all of that. At first I thought it was just family tension, you know, something that would blow over, but it didn’t.\n\nSo… yes, about nine months. Long enough that it’s not just one misunderstanding or one bad week. It’s been going on long enough that I can look back and see when things shifted.\n\nDoctor: Have you had the feeling that people can read your thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think they’re reading my thoughts exactly. It’s not like I believe someone can hear words in my head or anything like that.\n\nWhat I do think is that people are picking up things or being told things somehow, because there are these little things that keep happening. Like with my husband’s family — for months I’ve been trying to explain that they’re quietly interfering with our money and how people see us. Then a billing error shows up at work, or someone at church suddenly acts strange toward me, like they’ve heard something or they’re looking at me differently, and everyone wants to say it’s unrelated. But it doesn’t feel unrelated to me.\n\nSo, no, not reading my thoughts. It’s more like they know things or are spreading things, and no matter what records people pull up, I can see how it ties back to them.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that something outside you was putting thoughts into your mind?\n\nPatient: No, not like that. I don’t feel like someone is putting thoughts into my head or controlling what I think. My thoughts feel like my own.\n\nWhat I keep trying to explain is different. It’s that for months now, I’ve been seeing how my husband’s family is quietly messing with our money and our good name. It’s not some random idea that just appeared in my head from outside — it’s from things that keep happening. Like little billing errors showing up at work, or someone at church suddenly acting funny toward me, like they’ve heard something about me or they’re treating me differently. And then people want to pull up records or explain it away like it’s all separate, but I know it ties back to them.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say thoughts are being put into my mind. It’s more that I can see what’s going on, and it’s frustrating because other people keep acting like I’m the only one noticing it.\n\nDoctor: When you talk, do people ever tell you that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one idea to another in a way that doesn’t make sense to them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in the sense that people tell me I’m not making sense. I can be long-winded, I know that. My husband has said I explain things with too much detail, and sometimes people want me to get to the point faster, especially at work when everyone’s busy and they just want the short version.\n\nBut I don’t think people say I jump around in a way that doesn’t make sense. I may go back and fill in details because I feel like if I leave something out, then the whole thing gets misunderstood. So I’ll start with one part and then remember another part that matters, and I’ll circle back. To me it all connects, but I know it can probably sound like I’m giving more than they asked for.\n\nSo… hard to follow, maybe because I talk too much sometimes. But not because my words don’t make sense, at least not that anyone has really told me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that other people were controlling your actions?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anyone is controlling my actions. I mean, I make my own decisions, I go to work, I do what I need to do, and I don’t feel like someone is moving me around or making me say or do things.\n\nSometimes I might feel pressured by people, or like other people’s behavior affects what I have to do next — like if someone creates a problem, then I have to respond to it — but that’s not the same as them controlling my body or my actions. I know when I choose to do something. So no, I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nDoctor: If someone shows you evidence that your husband’s family was not involved in one of these incidents, are you able to accept that for that incident?\n\nPatient: Well… I can look at whatever someone wants to show me, and I’m not saying every single paper in front of me is fake or anything like that. If there’s a record that says one particular charge was entered by this person or that person, I can understand what the record says for that one little piece.\n\nBut what frustrates me is people act like showing me one record should make me ignore the whole pattern. These things have been happening for months — little billing errors showing up at work, people suddenly acting strange toward me at church, the way our money and our reputation seem to keep getting touched in these quiet ways. So if someone says, “Here, this proves they weren’t involved in this one incident,” I might say, fine, maybe not directly on that one paper or in that one step. But that doesn’t mean they weren’t behind it somehow, or that it doesn’t still tie back to them.\n\nThat’s the part people don’t seem to understand. They want to separate everything out like it’s all random, but when you’re the one living it, you see how it connects. So I can accept a detail, maybe, but I don’t just drop the whole thing because someone pulls up a record.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in being around other people or in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, not exactly. I mean, I still do things. I get up and go to work, and I handle my billing clerk duties. I may be tired of dealing with certain people, but I’m not just sitting at home doing nothing. I keep the house going, I take care of what needs taking care of.\n\nAnd I still like my own things, like gardening. That’s actually calming for me, because plants don’t twist things around, you know? And painting, too — when I have the time and the right frame of mind, I still enjoy that. So I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in everything or stopped wanting to do anything.\n\nWhere it gets difficult is more when I’m around people and I try to warn them about what my in-laws are doing. That’s when it changes. People get uncomfortable, or they act like I’m being dramatic, and then it makes me not want to keep explaining myself over and over. So it’s not that I don’t care about people or activities anymore. It’s more that some situations become hard because of how people react when I bring up what’s been going on.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, have you generally tended to mistrust other people and suspect that they might be trying to harm or deceive you?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think I was always like this. I mean, I’ve never been stupid about people — if someone seems dishonest, I notice it — but I wasn’t going around suspecting everybody or thinking people were out to get me.\n\nThis really started about nine months ago, after all that trouble with my husband’s family over money and the holiday expectations. That’s when things changed. Before that, I could get annoyed with people like anyone else, but I didn’t have this constant feeling that I had to watch what they were doing or figure out what was behind things. It’s been since that situation with them that I started seeing how these things were connecting.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt emotionally flat or unable to show much feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Emotionally flat? I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I still feel things, definitely. I get upset, I get frustrated, I can laugh if something is actually funny, and I still care about what’s happening around me.\n\nMaybe I don’t always show everything the way people expect me to. Sometimes if I’m worn out or if I feel like someone is judging me, I might get quieter, but that’s not the same as not feeling anything. Inside I’m usually feeling plenty. So no, I haven’t really noticed being unable to show feeling. I may be guarded at times, but not flat.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I can be long-winded, I know that. My husband has said I explain things with too much detail, and sometimes people want me to get to the point faster, especially at work when everyone’s busy and they just want the short version.\n\nBut I don’t think people say I jump around in a way that doesn’t make sense. I may go back and fill in details because I feel like if I leave something out, then the whole thing gets misunderstood. So I’ll start with one part and then remember another part that matters, and I’ll circle back. To me it all connects, but I know it can probably sound like I’m giving more than they asked for.\n\nSo… hard to follow, maybe because I talk too much sometimes. But not because my words don’t make sense, at least not that anyone has really told me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that other people were controlling your actions?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anyone is controlling my actions. I mean, I make my own decisions, I go to work, I do what I need to do, and I don’t feel like someone is moving me around or making me say or do things.\n\nSometimes I might feel pressured by people, or like other people’s behavior affects what I have to do next — like if someone creates a problem, then I have to respond to it — but that’s not the same as them controlling my body or my actions. I know when I choose to do something. So no, I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nDoctor: If someone shows you evidence that your husband’s family was not involved in one of these incidents, are you able to accept that for that incident?\n\nPatient: Well… I can look at whatever someone wants to show me, and I’m not saying every single paper in front of me is fake or anything like that. If there’s a record that says one particular charge was entered by this person or that person, I can understand what the record says for that one little piece.\n\nBut what frustrates me is people act like showing me one record should make me ignore the whole pattern. These things have been happening for months — little billing errors showing up at work, people suddenly acting strange toward me at church, the way our money and our reputation seem to keep getting touched in these quiet ways. So if someone says, “Here, this proves they weren’t involved in this one incident,” I might say, fine, maybe not directly on that one paper or in that one step. But that doesn’t mean they weren’t behind it somehow, or that it doesn’t still tie back to them.\n\nThat’s the part people don’t seem to understand. They want to separate everything out like it’s all random, but when you’re the one living it, you see how it connects. So I can accept a detail, maybe, but I don’t just drop the whole thing because someone pulls up a record.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in being around other people or in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, not exactly. I mean, I still do things. I get up and go to work, and I handle my billing clerk duties. I may be tired of dealing with certain people, but I’m not just sitting at home doing nothing. I keep the house going, I take care of what needs taking care of.\n\nAnd I still like my own things, like gardening. That’s actually calming for me, because plants don’t twist things around, you know? And painting, too — when I have the time and the right frame of mind, I still enjoy that. So I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in everything or stopped wanting to do anything.\n\nWhere it gets difficult is more when I’m around people and I try to warn them about what my in-laws are doing. That’s when it changes. People get uncomfortable, or they act like I’m being dramatic, and then it makes me not want to keep explaining myself over and over. So it’s not that I don’t care about people or activities anymore. It’s more that some situations become hard because of how people react when I bring up what’s been going on.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, have you generally tended to mistrust other people and suspect that they might be trying to harm or deceive you?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think I was always like this. I mean, I’ve never been stupid about people — if someone seems dishonest, I notice it — but I wasn’t going around suspecting everybody or thinking people were out to get me.\n\nThis really started about nine months ago, after all that trouble with my husband’s family over money and the holiday expectations. That’s when things changed. Before that, I could get annoyed with people like anyone else, but I didn’t have this constant feeling that I had to watch what they were doing or figure out what was behind things. It’s been since that situation with them that I started seeing how these things were connecting.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt emotionally flat or unable to show much feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Emotionally flat? I don’t know that I’d put it that way. I still feel things, definitely. I get upset, I get frustrated, I can laugh if something is actually funny, and I still care about what’s happening around me.\n\nMaybe I don’t always show everything the way people expect me to. Sometimes if I’m worn out or if I feel like someone is judging me, I might get quieter, but that’s not the same as not feeling anything. Inside I’m usually feeling plenty. So no, I haven’t really noticed being unable to show feeling. I may be guarded at times, but not flat.\n\nDoctor: Did this start suddenly over a few days?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say it was suddenly over just a few days. It’s been going on for about nine months now, really since that whole trouble started with my husband’s family — the money issues and all the holiday expectations, all of that.\n\nIt wasn’t like I woke up one morning and everything was different. It built from there, but that’s when I’d say it started, around nine months ago.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t mean like… magic or something like that, if that’s what you’re asking. I’m not saying they have some impossible power. It’s more that they know how to work quietly, through people, through little situations, so by the time something happens it looks like nothing.\n\nLike with the money and the billing things at work — I’m a billing clerk, I know how records look when they’re right and when something is off. And when these little errors pop up, not huge enough for someone to say, “Here, this proves it,” but just enough to make me look careless, it’s hard for me to believe that’s all accidental. Then the same kind of thing happens socially, like at church, where someone who was perfectly normal with me suddenly acts cold or careful around me. People say, “Well, maybe they were just having a bad day,” but when it keeps lining up, you start to see the pattern.\n\nSo no, not controlling events in an impossible way. But influencing things, yes. Quietly. In ways other people don’t want to take seriously because they can’t put one piece of paper in front of me and say, “This is the proof.” But I know it ties back to my husband’s family. I’ve been living with it for months.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }

Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry. Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. My husband and I have argued more because he thinks I should just let it go, or he acts like I’m making something out of nothing, and that’s frustrating. And at church there have been some uncomfortable moments, because people seem to want me to stop bringing it up or pretend everything is fine. So socially, yes, it’s made things awkward. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t think so… I mean, not in the sense that people tell me I’m not making sense. I can be long-winded, I know that. My husband has said I explain things with too much detail, and sometimes people want me to get to the point faster, especially at work when everyone’s busy and they just want the short version.\n\nBut I don’t think people say I jump around in a way that doesn’t make sense. I may go back and fill in details because I feel like if I leave something out, then the whole thing gets misunderstood. So I’ll start with one part and then remember another part that matters, and I’ll circle back. To me it all connects, but I know it can probably sound like I’m giving more than they asked for.\n\nSo… hard to follow, maybe because I talk too much sometimes. But not because my words don’t make sense, at least not that anyone has really told me.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }
]

Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, not exactly. I mean, I still do things. I get up and go to work, and I handle my billing clerk duties. I may be tired of dealing with certain people, but I’m not just sitting at home doing nothing. I keep the house going, I take care of what needs taking care of.\n\nAnd I still like my own things, like gardening. That’s actually calming for me, because plants don’t twist things around, you know? And painting, too — when I have the time and the right frame of mind, I still enjoy that. So I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in everything or stopped wanting to do anything.\n\nWhere it gets difficult is more when I’m around people and I try to warn them about what my in-laws are doing. That’s when it changes. People get uncomfortable, or they act like I’m being dramatic, and then it makes me not want to keep explaining myself over and over. So it’s not that I don’t care about people or activities anymore. It’s more that some situations become hard because of how people react when I bring up what’s been going on.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.
